The following description:

- The Zkr extension adds the **SSEED** and **USEED** fields to the **mseccfg** CSR to control access to the **seed** CSR from U, S, or HS modes (see Privileged ISA specification).

- Implementations **may** implement **mseccfg** such that **[s,u]seed** is a read‑only constant value **0**.

- Software **may** discover if access to the **seed** CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a **1** to **[s,u]seed** and reading back the result.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR, and these fields control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes.  
RULE 2: Implementations may implement the mseccfg CSR such that the SSEED and USEED fields are read‑only constant zero.  
RULE 3: Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to the SSEED or USEED field of the mseccfg CSR and reading back the result.

The following description:

- The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option "allows one to draw a security boundary around a component in relation to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows, even if that component is not in M mode." This is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves. Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option permits drawing a security boundary around a component with respect to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows even when that component is not operating in M mode.  
RULE 2: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is useful for implementing trusted enclaves.  
RULE 3: Modules that use the direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option can enforce the complete key lifecycle from generation in the entropy source to zeroization without the key being transferred across the security boundary to external code.

The following description:

- **CSR address 0x747** – privilege **MRW** – name **mseccfg** – described as the *Machine security configuration register*.

- **CSR address 0x757** – privilege **MRW** – name **mseccfgh** – described as the *Upper 32 bits of mseccfg, RV32 only*.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The CSR named mseccfg resides at address 0x747.  
RULE 2: The mseccfg CSR is a Machine‑mode read/write (MRW) register.  
RULE 3: The architectural purpose of the mseccfg CSR is to serve as the Machine security configuration register.  
RULE 4: The upper 32 bits of the mseccfg register are accessed through the CSR named mseccfgh located at address 0x757.  
RULE 5: The mseccfgh CSR has MRW privilege and is defined only for RV32 implementations.  
RULE 6: The combination of the lower 32‑bit mseccfg CSR and the upper‑32‑bit mseccfgh CSR forms the complete mseccfg register on RV32.
======================================================= END - 3 ========================================================

The following description:

- **Register existence**:  
  *mseccfg is a 64‑bit read/write register.* It *exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented*; otherwise it is *reserved*.

- **Fields added by extensions**:  
  *The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR* to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

- **USEED field (Zkr)**:  
  *When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.*  
  *When USEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from U‑mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception.*  
  *If Zkr or U‑mode is not implemented, USEED is read‑only zero.*

- **SSEED field (Zkr)**:  
  *When SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.*  
  *When SSEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception.*  
  *If Zkr or S‑mode is not implemented, SSEED is read‑only zero.*

- **Interaction with the H extension**:  
  *When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS‑qualified instruction leads to a virtual‑instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal‑instruction exception.*

- **Entropy source access control (Table 20)**  

  | Mode | SSEED | USEED | Architectural effect |
  |------|-------|-------|-----------------------|
  | M    | –     | –     | The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal (with a CSR read‑write instruction). *Attempted read without a write raises an illegal‑instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits.* |
  | U    | –     | 0     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception. |
  | U    | –     | 1     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. *No exception is raised for read‑write.* |
  | S/HS | 0     | –     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception. |
  | S/HS | 1     | –     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. *No exception is raised for read‑write.* |
  | VS/VU| 0     | –     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception. |
  | VS/VU| 1     | –     | A read‑write seed access raises a virtual‑instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal‑instruction exception. |

- **Smepmp extension fields**:  
  *The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in mseccfg.*

  - **RLB (Rule Locking Bypass) field**:  
    *When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.*  
    *When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.*  
    *Vendors who don't need this functionality may hardwire this field to 0.*  
    *Using this functionality under normal operation, after the boot process is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M‑mode‑only rules.*

  - **MMWP (Machine‑Mode Allowlist Policy) field**:  
    *mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field.*  
    *This field changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule.*  
    *This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.*  
    *When set it changes the default PMP policy for M‑mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored.*

  - **MML (Machine Mode Lockdown) field**:  
    *mseccfg.MML is a WARL field.*  
    *This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.*  
    *When mseccfg.MML is set the system's behavior changes as follows*:

      1. *The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes: instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M‑mode‑only when set and S/U‑mode‑only when unset. The formerly reserved encoding of pmpcfg.RW=01, and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, now encode a Shared‑Region.*

         - *An M‑mode‑only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode. It also remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.*

         - *An S/U‑mode‑only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.*

         - *A Shared‑Region rule:*
           - *When pmpcfg.L is not set, it can be used for sharing data between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, so is not executable. M‑mode has read/write access; S/U‑mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.*
           - *When pmpcfg.L is set, it can be used for sharing code between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, so is not writable. Both M‑mode and S/U‑mode have execute access; M‑mode also has read access if pmpcfg.X is set. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.*
           - *When pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, it can be used for sharing data between M‑mode and S/U‑mode where both modes only have read‑only access. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless mseccfg.RLB is set.*

      2. *Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M‑mode‑only or a locked Shared‑Region is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored, leaving pmpcfg unchanged. This restriction can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB (e.g., during the boot process).*

      3. *Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M‑mode‑only rule or a locked Shared‑Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U‑mode‑only rule is denied.*

      4. *If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.*

- **Smmpm extension – PMM field**:  
  *If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking (see Chapter 25) for M‑mode according to the values in Table 21.*  
  *If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read‑only zero.*  
  *The PMM field is read‑only zero for RV32.*

  **Table 21 – Legal values of PMM WARL field**

  | Value | Description |
  |-------|-------------|
  | 00    | Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0) |
  | 01    | Reserved |
  | 10    | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN – 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64) |
  | 11    | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN – 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64) |

- **Zicfilp extension – MLPE field**:  
  *When MLPE field is 1, Zicfilp extension is enabled in M‑mode.*  
  *When MLPE field is 0, Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M‑mode and the following rules apply to M‑mode:*  
    - *The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO_LP_EXPECTED.*  
    - *The LPAD instruction operates as a no‑op.*

- **XLEN‑specific alias register**:  
  *When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is a 32‑bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.*  
  *Register mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64‑bit read/write register.  
RULE 2: mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented; otherwise it is reserved.  
RULE 3: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.  
RULE 4: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 5: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from U‑mode is allowed.  
RULE 6: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, all other types of accesses to the seed CSR from U‑mode raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 7: If Zkr or U‑mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read‑only zero.  
RULE 8: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 9: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read‑write access to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode is allowed.  
RULE 10: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, all other types of accesses to the seed CSR from S‑/HS‑mode raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 11: If Zkr or S‑mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read‑only zero.  
RULE 12: When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS‑qualified instruction leads to a virtual‑instruction exception in VS and VU modes.  
RULE 13: When the H extension is also implemented, all other types of accesses to the seed CSR raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 14: In machine mode, mseccfg does not restrict access to the seed CSR; the seed CSR is always available as a normal read‑write CSR and a read without a write raises an illegal‑instruction exception regardless of mseccfg field values.  
RULE 15: In U‑mode with mseccfg.USEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 16: In U‑mode with mseccfg.USEED = 1, the seed CSR is accessible as normal and no exception is raised for read‑write accesses.  
RULE 17: In S‑/HS‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 18: In S‑/HS‑mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 1, the seed CSR is accessible as normal and no exception is raised for read‑write accesses.  
RULE 19: In VS/VU mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 0, any seed CSR access raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 20: In VS/VU mode with mseccfg.SSEED = 1, a read‑write seed access raises a virtual‑instruction exception while other access types raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 21: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in mseccfg.  
RULE 22: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.  
RULE 23: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and any pmpcfg.L is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.  
RULE 24: Software should avoid using mseccfg.RLB after the boot process is completed because it weakens the protection of M‑mode‑only rules.  
RULE 25: mseccfg.MMWP is a WARL field.  
RULE 26: mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule.  
RULE 27: mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.  
RULE 28: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, the default PMP policy for M‑mode for unmatched regions becomes denied instead of ignored.  
RULE 29: mseccfg.MML is a WARL field.  
RULE 30: mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.  
RULE 31: When mseccfg.MML is set, the meaning of pmpcfg.L changes so that it marks a rule as M‑mode‑only when set and as S/U‑mode‑only when unset.  
RULE 32: When mseccfg.MML is set, the previously reserved encoding pmpcfg.RW = 01 and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX = 1111 now encode a Shared‑Region.  
RULE 33: An M‑mode‑only rule (when mseccfg.MML is set) is enforced on Machine mode, denied on Supervisor and User modes, and remains locked so that any further modifications to its configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 34: An S/U‑mode‑only rule (when mseccfg.MML is set) is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.  
RULE 35: A Shared‑Region rule with pmpcfg.L not set (when mseccfg.MML is set) permits data sharing between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, is not executable, gives M‑mode read/write access, and gives S/U‑mode read access unless pmpcfg.X is set, in which case S/U‑mode has read/write access.  
RULE 36: A Shared‑Region rule with pmpcfg.L set (when mseccfg.MML is set) permits code sharing between M‑mode and S/U‑mode, is not writable, gives both modes execute access, gives M‑mode read access if pmpcfg.X is set, and remains locked unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 37: A Shared‑Region rule with pmpcfg.LRWX = 1111 (when mseccfg.MML is set) permits data sharing between M‑mode and S/U‑mode with both modes read‑only access and remains locked unless mseccfg.RLB is set.  
RULE 38: Adding a pmpcfg write that would create an executable M‑mode‑only or a locked Shared‑Region rule is not possible; such writes are ignored and pmpcfg remains unchanged.  
RULE 39: The restriction on adding executable M‑mode‑only or locked Shared‑Region rules can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB.  
RULE 40: Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M‑mode‑only rule or a locked Shared‑Region rule with executable privileges.  
RULE 41: Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U‑mode‑only rule is denied.  
RULE 42: If mseccfg.MML is not set, the encoding pmpcfg.RW = 01 remains reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 43: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field in mseccfg enables or disables pointer masking for M‑mode according to Table 21 values.  
RULE 44: If the Smmpm extension is not implemented, the PMM field in mseccfg is read‑only zero.  
RULE 45: For RV32, the PMM field in mseccfg is read‑only zero.  
RULE 46: PMM field value 00 in mseccfg indicates pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0).  
RULE 47: PMM field value 10 in mseccfg indicates pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN − 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64).  
RULE 48: PMM field value 11 in mseccfg indicates pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN − 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64).  
RULE 49: When the Zicfilp extension is present, the MLPE field in mseccfg being 1 enables the Zicfilp extension in M‑mode.  
RULE 50: When the MLPE field in mseccfg is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M‑mode and the hart does not update the ELP state, which remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 51: When the MLPE field in mseccfg is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no‑op.  
RULE 52: When XLEN = 32, mseccfgh is a 32‑bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.  
RULE 53: The mseccfgh register exists when XLEN = 32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN = 64.

The following description:

- The extension introduces the **RLB**, **MMWP**, and **MML** fields in the **mseccfg** CSR.  
- **Physical memory protection rules when `mseccfg.MML` is set to 1** are given by the truth table below. For each row, the condition is the combination of the four bits **L**, **R**, **W**, **X** in **pmpcfg**, and the effect is the "Result" column for the corresponding privilege mode (MMode or S/U Mode).

| Condition (pmpcfg bits) | MMode Result | S/U Mode Result |
|--------------------------|--------------|-----------------|
| L = 0, R = 0, W = 0, X = 0 | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) |
| L = 0, R = 0, W = 0, X = 1 | Access Exception | Execute‑only region |
| L = 0, R = 0, W = 1, X = 0 | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read‑only on S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read‑only on S/U mode |
| L = 0, R = 0, W = 1, X = 1 | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write for both M and S/U mode |
| L = 0, R = 1, W = 0, X = 0 | Access Exception | Read‑only region |
| L = 0, R = 1, W = 0, X = 1 | Access Exception | Read/Execute region |
| L = 0, R = 1, W = 1, X = 0 | Access Exception | Read/Write region |
| L = 0, R = 1, W = 1, X = 1 | Access Exception | Read/Write/Execute region |
| L = 1, R = 0, W = 0, X = 0 | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) |
| L = 1, R = 0, W = 0, X = 1 | Locked Execute‑only region* | Access Exception |
| L = 1, R = 0, W = 1, X = 0 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both M and S/U mode.* |
| L = 1, R = 0, W = 1, X = 1 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* |
| L = 1, R = 1, W = 0, X = 0 | Locked Read‑only region* | Access Exception |
| L = 1, R = 1, W = 0, X = 1 | Locked Read/Execute region* | Access Exception |
| L = 1, R = 1, W = 1, X = 0 | Locked Read/Write region* | Access Exception |
| L = 1, R = 1, W = 1, X = 1 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.* | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both M and S/U mode.* |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mseccfg CSR defines the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields.  
RULE 2: When mseccfg.MML = 1 and the PMP configuration bits L, R, W, X are all zero, any access in either M‑mode or S/U‑mode is an inaccessible region that raises an access exception.  
RULE 3: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 0, W = 0, X = 1, an access in M‑mode raises an access exception while an access in S/U‑mode is permitted only as an execute‑only region.  
RULE 4: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 0, W = 1, X = 0, M‑mode accesses are allowed to read and write the shared data region, and S/U‑mode accesses are allowed to read only.  
RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 0, W = 1, X = 1, both M‑mode and S/U‑mode accesses are allowed to read and write the shared data region.  
RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 1, W = 0, X = 0, an access in M‑mode raises an access exception while an access in S/U‑mode is permitted as a read‑only region.  
RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 1, W = 0, X = 1, an access in M‑mode raises an access exception while an access in S/U‑mode is permitted as a read‑execute region.  
RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 1, W = 1, X = 0, an access in M‑mode raises an access exception while an access in S/U‑mode is permitted as a read‑write region.  
RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 0, R = 1, W = 1, X = 1, an access in M‑mode raises an access exception while an access in S/U‑mode is permitted as a read‑write‑execute region.  
RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 0, W = 0, X = 0, any access in either M‑mode or S/U‑mode is a locked inaccessible region that raises an access exception.  
RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 0, W = 0, X = 1, M‑mode accesses are limited to a locked execute‑only region, whereas S/U‑mode accesses raise an access exception.  
RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 0, W = 1, X = 0, both M‑mode and S/U‑mode accesses are limited to a locked shared code region that permits execution only.  
RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 0, W = 1, X = 1, M‑mode accesses are permitted as read/execute in a locked shared code region, while S/U‑mode accesses are limited to execution only in the same locked region.  
RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 1, W = 0, X = 0, M‑mode accesses are permitted as a locked read‑only region, while S/U‑mode accesses raise an access exception.  
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 1, W = 0, X = 1, M‑mode accesses are permitted as a locked read/execute region, while S/U‑mode accesses raise an access exception.  
RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 1, W = 1, X = 0, M‑mode accesses are permitted as a locked read/write region, while S/U‑mode accesses raise an access exception.  
RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML = 1, L = 1, R = 1, W = 1, X = 1, both M‑mode and S/U‑mode accesses are limited to a locked shared data region that is read‑only for both privilege modes.

The following description:

- **When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set** and a **WRS.NTO instruction is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode**, **and the instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit**, **the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal‑instruction exception**.

- **When executing in VS or VU mode**, **if the VTW bit is set in hstatus**, **the TW bit in mstatus is clear**, **and the WRS.NTO instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit**, **the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual‑instruction exception**.

- **WRS.NTO is not specified to cause an illegal‑instruction exception if executed in U‑mode when the governing TW bit is 0**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the TW bit in mstatus is set, a WRS.NTO instruction is executed in a privilege mode other than M mode, and the instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, then the instruction causes an illegal‑instruction exception.  

RULE 2: If execution is in VS mode or VU mode, the VTW bit in hstatus is set, the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and a WRS.NTO instruction does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit, then the instruction causes a virtual‑instruction exception.  

RULE 3: When a WRS.NTO instruction is executed in U mode and the TW bit in mstatus is 0, the specification does not define an illegal‑instruction exception for that case.

The following description:

- **Location and shadowing**  
  *"A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9]."*  
  -> The VS field occupies bits 10‑9 of **mstatus** and the same bits of **sstatus** contain a shadow copy of this field.

- **Illegal‑instruction exception when VS = Off**  
  *"Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off."*  
  -> Condition: `mstatus.VS == Off` and the execution attempts a vector instruction **or** accesses a vector CSR.  
  -> Effect: an *illegal‑instruction* exception is raised.

- **Transition to Dirty on vector‑state‑changing instruction**  
  *"When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty."*  
  -> Condition: `mstatus.VS == Initial` **or** `mstatus.VS == Clean` **and** an instruction that changes vector state (including accesses to vector CSRs) is executed.  
  -> Effect: `mstatus.VS` is set to `Dirty`.

- **Implementation‑defined transition to Dirty**  
  *"Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state."*  
  -> Condition: Implementation‑defined; may occur while `mstatus.VS` is `Initial` **or** `Clean`, regardless of whether a vector‑state‑changing instruction is executed.  
  -> Effect: `mstatus.VS` is set to `Dirty`.

- **SD bit relationship**  
  *"If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications."*  
  -> Condition: `mstatus.VS == Dirty`.  
  -> Effect: `mstatus.SD` is set to `1`.  
  -> Condition: `mstatus.VS != Dirty`.  
  -> Effect: `mstatus.SD` follows the rules defined elsewhere for the SD bit.

- **Existence of VS independent of misa.V**  
  *"Implementations may have a writable misa.V field. Analogous to the way in which the floating‑point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear."*  
  -> Condition: `misa.V` is clear (and may be writable).  
  -> Effect: The `mstatus.VS` field **may still exist**.

- **Purpose of allowing VS when misa.V is clear**  
  *"Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear, enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V."*  
  -> Condition: `misa.V` is clear.  
  -> Effect: `mstatus.VS` existence enables vector emulation and simplifies its handling.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The VS field occupies bits 10-9 of mstatus.  
RULE 2: Bits 10-9 of sstatus contain a shadow copy of the VS field of mstatus.  
RULE 3: When mstatus.VS is Off, executing any vector instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.  
RULE 4: When mstatus.VS is Off, accessing a vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.  
RULE 5: When mstatus.VS is Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including accesses to vector CSRs, sets mstatus.VS to Dirty.  
RULE 6: Implementations may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.  
RULE 7: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is set to 1.  
RULE 8: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD follows the rules defined elsewhere for the SD bit.  
RULE 9: The mstatus.VS field may exist even when misa.V is clear.  
RULE 10: When misa.V is clear, the existence of mstatus.VS enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.

The following description:

- **When V=1, attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal‑instruction exception when either vsstatus.VS or mstatus.VS is set to Off.**  
  *Condition:* V=1 **and** (vsstatus.VS = Off **or** mstatus.VS = Off)  
  *Effect:* execution of any vector instruction or any access to a vector CSR causes an illegal‑instruction exception.

- **When V=1 and neither vsstatus.VS nor mstatus.VS is set to Off, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.**  
  *Condition:* V=1 **and** (vsstatus.VS ≠ Off **and** mstatus.VS ≠ Off) **and** an instruction that changes vector state (including vector CSR accesses) is executed  
  *Effect:* mstatus.VS <- Dirty **and** vsstatus.VS <- Dirty.

- **Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.**  
  *Condition:* (implementation‑defined) — no required condition; may occur at any time, irrespective of vector‑state changes.  
  *Effect:* mstatus.VS may be set to Dirty from Initial or Clean.

- **If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.**  
  *Condition:* mstatus.VS = Dirty  
  *Effect:* mstatus.SD <- 1.  
  *Condition:* mstatus.VS ≠ Dirty  
  *Effect:* mstatus.SD is set according to the existing specifications (i.e., as defined for other status fields).
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1 and either vsstatus.VS or mstatus.VS is Off, any attempt to execute a vector instruction or to access a vector CSR must raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: When V=1 and both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are not Off, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including accesses to vector CSRs, must set both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.  
RULE 3: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, then mstatus.SD must be 1.  
RULE 4: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, then mstatus.SD is defined by the existing specifications for other status fields.

The following description:

- If the floating‑point unit status field **mstatus.FS** is **Off** then any attempt to execute a vector floating‑point instruction **will raise an illegal-instruction exception**.  

- Any vector floating‑point instruction that modifies any floating‑point extension state (i.e., floating‑point CSRs or f registers) **must set mstatus.FS to Dirty**.  

- If the hypervisor extension is implemented and **V=1**, the **vsstatus.FS** field is **additionally in effect** for vector floating‑point instructions.  

- If **vsstatus.FS** or **mstatus.FS** is **Off** then any attempt to execute a vector floating‑point instruction **will raise an illegal‑instruction exception**.  

- Any vector floating‑point instruction that modifies any floating‑point extension state (i.e., floating‑point CSRs or f registers) **must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating‑point instruction will raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: Any vector floating‑point instruction that modifies any floating‑point extension state must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.  
RULE 3: When the hypervisor extension is implemented and the V bit is 1, vector floating‑point instructions are additionally governed by the vsstatus.FS field alongside mstatus.FS.  
RULE 4: If either vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating‑point instruction will raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 5: Any vector floating‑point instruction that modifies any floating‑point extension state must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty.

The following description:

- The global interrupt‑enable bits **MIE** and **SIE** are located in the low‑order bits of **mstatus**, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.  

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode **x**, interrupts are globally enabled when **xIE = 1** and globally disabled when **xIE = 0**.  

- Interrupts for lower‑privilege modes (**w < x**) are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global **wIE** bit for the lower‑privilege mode.  

- Interrupts for higher‑privilege modes (**y > x**) are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global **yIE** bit for the higher‑privilege mode.  

- If supervisor mode is not implemented, then **SIE** and **SPIE** are read‑only 0.  

- Each privilege mode **x** that can respond to interrupts has a two‑level stack of interrupt‑enable bits and privilege modes stored in **mstatus**: **xPIE** holds the value of the interrupt‑enable bit active prior to the trap, and **xPP** holds the previous privilege mode.  

- When a trap is taken from privilege mode **y** into privilege mode **x**, **xPIE** is set to the value of **xIE**; **xIE** is set to 0; and **xPP** is set to **y**.  

- An **xRET** instruction (i.e., **MRET** or **SRET**) executes the following architectural effects on **mstatus**:  
  1. If **xPP** holds the value **y**, **xIE** is set to **xPIE**.  
  2. The privilege mode is changed to **y**.  
  3. **xPIE** is set to 1.  
  4. **xPP** is set to the least‑privileged supported mode (U if U‑mode is implemented, else M).  
  5. If **y ≠ M**, the **xRET** also sets **MPRV = 0**.  

- **xPP** fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode **x** and any implemented privilege mode lower than **x**.  

- If privilege mode **x** is not implemented, then **xPP** **must** be read‑only 0.  

- **M‑mode** software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to **MPP** then reading it back.  

- If the machine provides only U and M modes, only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either **00** or **11** in **MPP**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The global interrupt‑enable bits MIE and SIE reside in the low‑order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.  
RULE 2: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled if the xIE bit in mstatus equals 1 and globally disabled if that bit equals 0.  
RULE 3: For any lower‑privilege mode w (w < x), interrupts are always globally disabled in mstatus irrespective of the value of the wIE bit.  
RULE 4: For any higher‑privilege mode y (y > x), interrupts are always globally enabled in mstatus irrespective of the value of the yIE bit.  
RULE 5: If supervisor mode is not implemented, the SIE and SPIE fields in mstatus are read‑only zero.  
RULE 6: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus contains a two‑level stack consisting of the xPIE field that holds the interrupt‑enable value active before a trap and the xPP field that holds the previous privilege mode.  
RULE 7: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to the current value of xIE, the xIE field in mstatus is cleared to zero, and the xPP field in mstatus is written with the value y.  
RULE 8: An xRET instruction (MRET or SRET) sets the xIE field in mstatus to the value of xPIE when the xPP field holds the previous mode y.  
RULE 9: An xRET instruction updates the architectural privilege mode stored in mstatus to the value held in the xPP field.  
RULE 10: An xRET instruction sets the xPIE field in mstatus to 1.  
RULE 11: An xRET instruction writes the xPP field in mstatus with the least‑privileged supported mode, which is U if user mode is implemented otherwise M.  
RULE 12: If the previous privilege mode y is not M, an xRET instruction also clears the MPRV field in mstatus to 0.  
RULE 13: Each xPP field in mstatus is a WARL field that may contain only the privilege mode x or any implemented privilege mode lower than x.  
RULE 14: If privilege mode x is not implemented, the corresponding xPP field in mstatus must be read‑only zero.  
RULE 15: Machine‑mode software can test the implementation of a privilege mode by writing that mode into the MPP field of mstatus and then reading the field back.  
RULE 16: When only U and M privilege modes are provided, a single hardware storage bit in mstatus suffices to represent either the binary pattern 00 or 11 in the MPP field.

The following description:

- The **M‑mode‑disable‑trap (MDT) bit** is a WARL field introduced by the **Smdbltrp** extension.  
  * Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.

- **Effect of writing the MDT bit:**  
  * When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the **MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit** is cleared to 0.  
  * For RV64, this clearing occurs *regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit* by the same write.

- **Effect of writing the MIE bit:**  
  * The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write **if the MDT bit is already 0** or, for RV64, **if the MDT bit is being set to 0 by the same write**.  
  * For RV32, the MDT bit resides in **mstatush** and the MIE bit in the **mstatus** register.

- **Behaviour of the MDT bit on trap entry to M‑mode:**  
  * When a trap is to be taken into M‑mode and the MDT bit is currently 0, the MDT bit is then set to 1 and the trap is delivered as expected.  
  * If the MDT bit is already set to 1, the trap is classified as an **unexpected trap**.

- **Behaviour of the MDT bit for RNMI‑caused traps:**  
  * A trap caused by an RNMI does **not** set the MDT bit.  
  * When the **Smrnmi** extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is **not** considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.

- **Unexpected trap condition related to mnstatus.NMIE:**  
  * A trap that occurs while executing in M‑mode with **mnstatus.NMIE = 0** is an unexpected trap.

- **Instructions that modify the MDT bit:**  
  * The **MRET** and **SRET** instructions, when executed in M‑mode, set the MDT bit to 0.  
  * The **MNRET** instruction (provided by the Smrnmi extension) sets the MDT bit to 0 *if the new privilege mode is not M*.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: In mstatus, the MDT bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.  
RULE 2: Upon reset, the MDT field in mstatus is set to 1.  
RULE 3: When the MDT bit in mstatus is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit in mstatus is cleared to 0.  
RULE 4: On RV64, writing the MDT bit to 1 in mstatus clears the MIE bit to 0 regardless of any value supplied for the MIE bit in the same CSR write.  
RULE 5: The MIE bit in mstatus may be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write only if the MDT bit in mstatus is already 0, or on RV64 only if the same CSR write also sets the MDT bit to 0.  
RULE 6: On RV32, the MDT bit resides in the mstatush register while the MIE bit resides in the mstatus register.  
RULE 7: If a trap is taken into M‑mode while the MDT bit in mstatus is 0, the MDT bit is set to 1 before the trap is delivered.  
RULE 8: If a trap is taken into M‑mode while the MDT bit in mstatus is already 1, the trap is classified as an unexpected trap.  
RULE 9: A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit in mstatus.  
RULE 10: If the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap regardless of the MDT bit value in mstatus.  
RULE 11: When executed in M‑mode, the MRET instruction sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0.  
RULE 12: When executed in M‑mode, the SRET instruction sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0.  
RULE 13: When the MNRET instruction (provided by the Smrnmi extension) is executed, it sets the MDT bit in mstatus to 0 if the resulting privilege mode is not M.

The following description:

- For RV64 harts, the **SXL** and **UXL** fields are **WARL** fields that control the value of **XLEN** for **S‑mode** and **U‑mode**, respectively.  
  - The encoding of these fields is the same as the **MXL** field of **misa**, as shown in Table 11.  
  - The effective XLEN in S‑mode and U‑mode are termed **SXLEN** and **UXLEN**, respectively.

- **When MXLEN = 32**:  
  - The **SXL** and **UXL** fields do **not exist**.  
  - **SXLEN = 32** and **UXLEN = 32**.

- **When MXLEN = 64**:  
  - **If S‑mode is not supported**, **SXL** is **read‑only zero**.  
  - **If S‑mode is supported**, **SXL** is a **WARL** field that encodes the current value of **SXLEN**.  
    - *In particular*, an implementation **may** make **SXL** be a **read‑only** field whose value always ensures that **SXLEN = MXLEN**.  

  - **If U‑mode is not supported**, **UXL** is **read‑only zero**.  
  - **If U‑mode is supported**, **UXL** is a **WARL** field that encodes the current value of **UXLEN**.  
    - *In particular*, an implementation **may** make **UXL** be a **read‑only** field whose value always ensures that **UXLEN = MXLEN** or **UXLEN = SXLEN**.

- **If S‑mode is implemented**, the **set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN > SXLEN**.

- **Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN**, the following architectural effects apply:  
  - All operations **must ignore** source‑operand register bits above the configured XLEN, and **must sign‑extend** results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.  
  - PC bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the PC is written it **must** be sign‑extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.  

- The architecture **imposes no checks** that lower‑privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next‑higher privilege mode; **machine operation is well‑defined** even when such settings occur.

- Some **HINT** instructions are encoded as integer computational instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value (e.g., `c.addi x8,0`).  
  - **When such a HINT is executed with XLEN < MXLEN** and **bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN‑1**, it is **implementation‑defined** whether bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN‑1.  

- The definition allows implementations to **elide register write‑back** for some HINTs while executing other HINTs like regular integer computational instructions. The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: In mstatus for RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the XLEN value used in S‑mode and U‑mode respectively.  
RULE 2: The encoding of the SXL and UXL fields in mstatus matches the encoding of the MXL field in the misa register.  
RULE 3: When MXLEN equals 32, the mstatus register does not contain the SXL or UXL fields.  
RULE 4: When MXLEN equals 32, the mstatus register lacks SXL and UXL fields and the effective XLEN values SXLEN and UXLEN are fixed at 32.  
RULE 5: If S‑mode is not supported and MXLEN = 64, the SXL field in mstatus is a read‑only zero.  
RULE 6: If S‑mode is supported and MXLEN = 64, the SXL field in mstatus is a WARL field that encodes the current SXLEN value.  
RULE 7: An implementation may make the SXL field in mstatus read‑only such that its value always forces SXLEN to equal MXLEN.  
RULE 8: If U‑mode is not supported and MXLEN = 64, the UXL field in mstatus is a read‑only zero.  
RULE 9: If U‑mode is supported and MXLEN = 64, the UXL field in mstatus is a WARL field that encodes the current UXLEN value.  
RULE 10: An implementation may make the UXL field in mstatus read‑only such that its value always forces UXLEN to equal MXLEN or to equal SXLEN.  
RULE 11: When S‑mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field in mstatus may assume excludes any value that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.  
RULE 12: Whenever XLEN in any privilege mode is configured via mstatus to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source‑operand register bits above the configured XLEN and must sign‑extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.  
RULE 13: When XLEN in any privilege mode is configured via mstatus to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, PC bits above the configured XLEN are ignored and when the PC is written it must be sign‑extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.  
RULE 14: The architecture imposes no checks in mstatus that lower‑privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next‑higher privilege mode, and machine operation remains well‑defined even when such settings occur.  
RULE 15: When a HINT instruction that overwrites its destination register with its current value is executed with XLEN < MXLEN and the bits from MXLEN down to XLEN of the destination register are not all equal to bit XLEN‑1, it is implementation‑defined whether those bits are unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN‑1, as observed through the XLEN settings in mstatus.  
RULE 16: Implementations may elide the register write‑back for some HINT instructions while performing normal write‑back for other integer computational instructions, and this choice is observable only by privilege modes whose XLEN setting, as defined by mstatus, is greater than the current XLEN.
======================================================= END - 18 =======================================================

The following description:

- **MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) bit in mstatus**  
  - *Behavior*: "The MPRV bit modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute."  
  - *Condition*: **MPRV=0** -> "Explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode."  
  - *Condition*: **MPRV=1** -> "Load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to **MPP**."  
  - *Condition*: "Instruction address‑translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV."  
  - *Read‑only rule*: "MPRV is read‑only 0 if **U‑mode** is not supported."  
  - *Side‑effect of return instructions*: "An **MRET** or **SRET** instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than **M** also sets **MPRV=0**."  
  - *XLEN interaction*: "When **MPRV=1**, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current **XLEN** were set to **MPP**'s XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3."

- **MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) bit in mstatus**  
  - *Behavior*: "The MXR bit modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory."  
  - *Condition*: **MXR=0** -> "Only loads from pages marked readable (**R=1** in Figure 67) will succeed."  
  - *Condition*: **MXR=1** -> "Loads from pages marked either readable or executable (**R=1** or **X=1**) will succeed."  
  - *No‑effect condition*: "MXR has no effect when **page‑based virtual memory** is not in effect."  
  - *Read‑only rule*: "MXR is read‑only 0 if **S‑mode** is not supported."

- **SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) bit in mstatus**  
  - *Behavior*: "The SUM bit modifies the privilege with which **S‑mode** loads and stores access virtual memory."  
  - *Condition*: **SUM=0** -> "S‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by **U‑mode** (**U=1** in Figure 67) will fault."  
  - *Condition*: **SUM=1** -> "These accesses are permitted."  
  - *No‑effect condition*: "SUM has no effect when **page‑based virtual memory** is not in effect."  
  - *Special case*: "While SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in **S‑mode**, it is in effect when **MPRV=1** and **MPP=S**."  
  - *Read‑only rule*: "SUM is read‑only 0 if **S‑mode** is not supported **or** if **satp.MODE** is read‑only 0."

- **Interaction of MXR and SUM with other mechanisms**  
  - "The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in **page‑table entries**. In particular, **they have no impact on whether access‑fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP**."  

*(All statements above are reproduced verbatim from the source text, preserving the original normative language and conditions.)*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MPRV field in mstatus modifies the effective privilege mode at which explicit memory accesses execute.  
RULE 2: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 0, explicit memory accesses behave normally using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.  
RULE 3: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are translated, protected, and have endianness applied as if the current privilege mode were set to the value in the MPP field of mstatus.  
RULE 4: Instruction address‑translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of the MPRV field in mstatus.  
RULE 5: The MPRV field in mstatus is read‑only zero if user mode is not supported.  
RULE 6: Executing an MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than machine mode also sets the MPRV field in mstatus to zero.  
RULE 7: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were the XLEN associated with the MPP field of mstatus.  
RULE 8: The MXR field in mstatus modifies the privilege with which load instructions access virtual memory.  
RULE 9: When the MXR field in mstatus is 0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1) will succeed.  
RULE 10: When the MXR field in mstatus is 1, loads from pages marked readable (R=1) or executable (X=1) will succeed.  
RULE 11: The MXR field in mstatus has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
RULE 12: The MXR field in mstatus is read‑only zero if supervisor mode is not supported.  
RULE 13: The SUM field in mstatus modifies the privilege with which supervisor‑mode loads and stores access virtual memory.  
RULE 14: When the SUM field in mstatus is 0, supervisor‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by user mode (U=1) will fault.  
RULE 15: When the SUM field in mstatus is 1, supervisor‑mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by user mode (U=1) are permitted.  
RULE 16: The SUM field in mstatus has no effect when page‑based virtual memory is not in effect.  
RULE 17: The SUM field in mstatus is in effect when MPRV is 1 and the MPP field is set to supervisor mode, even if the current execution mode is not supervisor mode.  
RULE 18: The SUM field in mstatus is read‑only zero if supervisor mode is not supported or if the satp.MODE field is read‑only zero.  
RULE 19: The MXR and SUM fields in mstatus only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page‑table entries.  
RULE 20: The MXR and SUM fields in mstatus have no impact on whether access‑fault exceptions are raised due to physical memory attributes or the physical memory protection subsystem.

The following description:

- The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in **mstatus** and **mstatush** are **WARL** fields that **control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches**.  
- **Instruction fetches are always little-endian**.

- **MBE controls whether non‑instruction‑fetch memory accesses made from M‑mode (assuming **`mstatus.MPRV=0`**) are little‑endian (MBE=0) or big‑endian (MBE=1).**

- **If S‑mode is not supported, SBE is read‑only 0.**  
  **Otherwise, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S‑mode are little‑endian (SBE=0) or big‑endian (SBE=1).**

- **If U‑mode is not supported, UBE is read‑only 0.**  
  **Otherwise, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U‑mode are little‑endian (UBE=0) or big‑endian (UBE=1).**

- **For implicit accesses to supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.**

- **Since changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of these data structures, if any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M‑mode software **MUST** follow such a change to SBE by executing an **SFENCE.VMA** instruction with **`rs1 = x0`** and **`rs2 = x0`**.**

- **If S‑mode is supported, an implementation **may** make SBE be a read‑only copy of MBE.**

- **If U‑mode is supported, an implementation **may** make UBE be a read‑only copy of either MBE or SBE.**

- **An implementation supports only little‑endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read‑only 0.**

- **An implementation supports only big‑endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read‑only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read‑only 1 when S‑mode and U‑mode are supported.**
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus (and mstatush) are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.  
RULE 2: Instruction fetches are always little‑endian regardless of the values of the fields in mstatus.  
RULE 3: When mstatus.MPRV=0, the MBE bit in mstatus determines whether non‑instruction‑fetch memory accesses from M‑mode are little‑endian (MBE=0) or big‑endian (MBE=1).  
RULE 4: If supervisor mode is not supported, the SBE bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 5: When supervisor mode is supported, the SBE bit in mstatus determines whether explicit load and store accesses from S‑mode are little‑endian (SBE=0) or big‑endian (SBE=1).  
RULE 6: If user mode is not supported, the UBE bit in mstatus is read‑only 0.  
RULE 7: When user mode is supported, the UBE bit in mstatus determines whether explicit load and store accesses from U‑mode are little‑endian (UBE=0) or big‑endian (UBE=1).  
RULE 8: For implicit accesses to supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures, such as page tables, the endianness is always controlled by the SBE bit in mstatus.  
RULE 9: If any supervisor‑level memory‑management data structures remain in use across a change to the SBE bit in mstatus, M‑mode software MUST follow the change by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1=x0 and rs2=x0.  
RULE 10: An implementation supports only little‑endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) when the MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus are all read‑only 0.  
RULE 11: An implementation supports only big‑endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) when MBE is read‑only 1 and, if supervisor mode and user mode are supported, SBE and UBE are each read‑only 1 in mstatus.

The following description:

- **TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) field** – a WARL field in the mstatus register.  
  - **When TVM = 1:** attempts to read or write the **satp** CSR or to execute an **SFENCE.VMA** or **SINVAL.VMA** instruction while executing in **S‑mode** will raise an **illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - **When TVM = 0:** these operations are permitted in **S‑mode**.  
  - **TVM is read‑only 0** when **S‑mode is not supported**.

- **TW (Timeout Wait) field** – a WARL field in the mstatus register.  
  - **When TW = 0:** the **WFI** instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.  
  - **When TW = 1:** if **WFI** is executed in any less‑privileged mode, and it does not complete within an implementation‑specific, bounded time limit, the **WFI** instruction causes an **illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - **Implementation‑specific variant:** an implementation may have **WFI** always raise an **illegal‑instruction exception** in modes less privileged than M when **TW = 1**, even if there are pending globally‑disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.  
  - **TW is read‑only 0** when there are no modes less privileged than M.  
  - **Additional rule when S‑mode is implemented:** executing **WFI** in **U‑mode** causes an **illegal‑instruction exception**, regardless of the value of the **TW** bit, unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific, bounded time limit.

- **TSR (Trap SRET) field** – a WARL field in the mstatus register.  
  - **When TSR = 1:** attempts to execute **SRET** while executing in **S‑mode** will raise an **illegal‑instruction exception**.  
  - **When TSR = 0:** this operation is permitted in **S‑mode**.  
  - **TSR is read‑only 0** when **S‑mode is not supported**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The TVM field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 2: When the TVM field in mstatus is 1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR or to execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: When the TVM field in mstatus is 0, reads or writes of the satp CSR and execution of SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instructions are permitted in S-mode.  
RULE 4: The TVM field in mstatus is read‑only 0 when S-mode is not supported.  
RULE 5: The TW field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 6: When the TW field in mstatus is 0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M unless prevented for some other reason.  
RULE 7: When the TW field in mstatus is 1 and WFI is executed in any less‑privileged mode, WFI causes an illegal‑instruction exception if it does not complete within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit.  
RULE 8: The TW field in mstatus is read‑only 0 when there are no modes less privileged than M.  
RULE 9: When S-mode is implemented, execution of WFI in U-mode causes an illegal‑instruction exception regardless of the TW field in mstatus unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific bounded time limit.  
RULE 10: The TSR field in mstatus is a WARL field.  
RULE 11: When the TSR field in mstatus is 1, attempts to execute SRET while executing in S-mode raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 12: When the TSR field in mstatus is 0, execution of SRET is permitted in S-mode.  
RULE 13: The TSR field in mstatus is read‑only 0 when S-mode is not supported.

The following description:

**mstatus fields**

- **FS[1:0]** – a WARL field in mstatus that "encodes the status of the floating‑point unit state, including the floating‑point registers f0‑f31 and the CSRs fcsr, frm, and fflags."  
- **VS[1:0]** – a WARL field in mstatus that "encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0‑v31 and the CSRs vcsr, vxrm, vxsat, vstart, vl, vtype, and vlenb."  
- **XS[1:0]** – a read‑only field in mstatus that "encodes the status of additional user‑mode extensions and associated state."  
- **SD** – a read‑only bit in mstatus that "summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory."  The text defines it explicitly as  
  `SD = (FS == 0b11) OR (VS == 0b11) OR (XS == 0b11)`.

**Read‑only conditions for the status fields**

- "If the F extension is implemented, the FS field **shall not** be read‑only zero."
- "If neither the F extension nor S‑mode is implemented, then FS **is** read‑only zero."
- "If S‑mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS **may optionally** be read‑only zero."
- "Implementations with S‑mode but without the F extension are permitted, but not required, to make the FS field be read‑only zero."
- "If the v registers are implemented, the VS field **shall not** be read‑only zero."
- "If neither the v registers nor S‑mode is implemented, then VS **is** read‑only zero."
- "If S‑mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS **may optionally** be read‑only zero."
- "In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field **is** read‑only zero."
- "If FS, XS, and VS are all read‑only zero, then SD **is also always zero**."

**Encoding of the status values (Table 13)**  

| Status value (binary) | FS and VS meaning | XS meaning |
|-----------------------|-------------------|------------|
| 0 | Off | All off |
| 1 | Initial | None dirty or clean, some on |
| 2 | Clean | None dirty, some clean |
| 3 | Dirty | Some dirty |

**Behavior of the Off state**

- "When an extension's status is set to Off, **any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal‑instruction exception**."

**Behavior of the other states (Initial, Clean, Dirty)**  

- "When the status is Initial, the corresponding state **should have an initial constant value**."
- "When the status is Clean, the corresponding state **is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap**."
- "When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state **has potentially been modified since the last context save**."

**Context‑save and context‑restore actions (privileged code)**  

- "During a context save, the responsible privileged code **need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean**."
- "During a context restore, the context **need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore)**."
- "If the status is Initial, the context **must be set to an initial constant value on context restore** to avoid a security hole; this can be done without accessing memory."
- "The FS and XS fields **are read by the privileged code before saving the context**."
- "The FS field **is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context**, while the XS field **is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions**."
- "The status fields **will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode**."

**Effect of changing FS or VS settings**

- "Changing the setting of FS **has no effect on the contents of the floating‑point register state**. In particular, **setting FS=Off does not destroy the state, nor does setting FS=Initial clear the contents**."
- "Similarly, the setting of VS **has no effect on the contents of the vector register state**. Other extensions, however, might not preserve state when set to Off."

**Imprecise dirtiness tracking (floating‑point)**  

- "Implementations **may choose** to track the dirtiness of the floating‑point register state imprecisely by **reporting the state to be dirty even when it has not been modified**."
- "On some implementations, **some instructions that do not mutate the floating‑point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty**."
- "On other implementations, **dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty**."
- "This definition of FS **does not disallow** setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation."
- "If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating‑point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it **is implementation‑defined whether FS transitions to Dirty**."

**Imprecise dirtiness tracking (vector)**  

- "Implementations **may choose** to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean."
- "When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it **is implementation‑defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty**."

**Machine‑Supervisor sharing**

- "Machine and Supervisor modes **share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits**."
- "Supervisor‑level software normally **uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly** to record the status with respect to the supervisor‑level saved context."
- "Machine‑level software **must be more conservative** in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context."

**State‑transition table (Table 14) – explicit rules for each current state and each action**

*All entries are read directly from Table 14; each row is a separate rule.*

1. **Action:** "At context save in privileged code"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Save state? No" -> **Effect:** No state is saved; next state remains Off.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Save state? No" -> **Effect:** No state is saved; next state remains Initial.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Save state? No" -> **Effect:** No state is saved; next state remains Clean.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Save state? Yes" -> **Effect:** State is saved; next state becomes Clean.

2. **Action:** "At context restore in privileged code"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Restore state? No" -> **Effect:** No state is restored; next state remains Off.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Restore state? Yes, to initial" -> **Effect:** Context is set to an initial constant value; next state remains Initial.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Restore state? Yes, from memory" -> **Effect:** State is loaded from memory; next state remains Clean.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Restore state? N/A" -> **Effect:** No defined restore (the text marks N/A).

3. **Action:** "Execute instruction to read state"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Action? Exception" -> **Effect:** Illegal‑instruction exception is raised.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Action? Execute" -> **Effect:** Instruction executes normally.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Action? Execute" -> **Effect:** Instruction executes normally.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Action? Execute" -> **Effect:** Instruction executes normally.

4. **Action:** "Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Action? Exception" -> **Effect:** Illegal‑instruction exception is raised.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Action? Execute Dirty" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Dirty and instruction executes.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Action? Execute Dirty" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Dirty and instruction executes.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Action? Execute Dirty" -> **Effect:** State remains Dirty and instruction executes.

5. **Action:** "Execute instruction to unconfigure unit"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Action? Exception" -> **Effect:** Illegal‑instruction exception is raised.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State remains Initial.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Initial.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Initial.

6. **Action:** "Execute instruction to disable unit"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Action? Execute Off" -> **Effect:** State remains Off (instruction executes).  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Action? Execute Off" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Off.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Action? Execute Off" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Off.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Action? Execute Off" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Off.

7. **Action:** "Execute instruction to enable unit"  
   - **Current state Off:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Initial.  
   - **Current state Initial:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State remains Initial.  
   - **Current state Clean:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Initial.  
   - **Current state Dirty:** "Action? Execute Initial" -> **Effect:** State transitions to Initial.

RULE 46: At a context save, if a status field in mstatus is Clean then no state is saved and the status remains Clean.  
RULE 47: At a context save, if a status field in mstatus is Dirty then the corresponding state is saved and the status is changed to Clean.  
RULE 48: At a context restore, if a status field in mstatus is Off then no state is restored and the status remains Off.  
RULE 49: At a context restore, if a status field in mstatus is Initial then the context is set to an initial constant value and the status remains Initial.  
RULE 50: At a context restore, if a status field in mstatus is Clean then the state is loaded from memory and the status remains Clean.  
RULE 51: At a context restore, if a status field in mstatus is Dirty then there is no defined restore action.

RULE 53: If a status field in mstatus is Initial, a read of the corresponding state executes normally.

RULE 55: If a status field in mstatus is Dirty, a read of the corresponding state executes normally.

RULE 57: If a status field in mstatus is Initial, executing an instruction that may modify the corresponding state causes the status to transition to Dirty and the instruction executes.  
RULE 58: If a status field in mstatus is Clean, executing an instruction that may modify the corresponding state causes the status to transition to Dirty and the instruction executes.  
RULE 59: If a status field in mstatus is Dirty, executing an instruction that may modify the corresponding state leaves the status Dirty and the instruction executes.  
RULE 60: If a status field in mstatus is Off, an attempt to unconfigure the unit raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 61: If a status field in mstatus is Initial, executing an unconfigure instruction leaves the status Initial.  
RULE 62: If a status field in mstatus is Clean, executing an unconfigure instruction transitions the status to Initial.  
RULE 63: If a status field in mstatus is Dirty, executing an unconfigure instruction transitions the status to Initial.

RULE 65: If a status field in mstatus is Initial, executing a disable instruction transitions the status to Off.

RULE 67: If a status field in mstatus is Dirty, executing a disable instruction transitions the status to Off.

RULE 69: If a status field in mstatus is Initial, executing an enable instruction leaves the status Initial.

The following description:

- An interrupt i will trap to M‑mode (causing the privilege mode to change to M‑mode) if all of the following are true:  
  (a) either the current privilege mode is M **and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set**, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M‑mode;  
  (b) bit i is set in both mip and mie; and  
  (c) if the register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

- The conditions for an interrupt trap to occur **must be evaluated immediately following the execution of an x RET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend (including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg).**
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When the current privilege mode is M, an interrupt i can trap to M‑mode only if the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set.  

RULE 2: The evaluation of interrupt trap conditions must be performed immediately after any explicit write to the mstatus CSR.

The following description:

- The **Wait for Interrupt (WFI)** instruction *may raise an illegal‑instruction exception* when **TW = 1** in **mstatus**.  

- The **operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE)**; i.e., the values of **MIE** and **SIE** in **mstatus** do not influence whether WFI stalls the hart or resumes execution.

- **WFI is also required to resume execution for locally enabled interrupts pending at any privilege level, regardless of the global interrupt enable at each privilege level.** This requirement follows from the rule that the operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits (MIE and SIE) in **mstatus**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The WFI instruction may raise an illegal‑instruction exception when the TW field in mstatus is 1.  
RULE 2: The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the MIE and SIE bits in mstatus, and the values of MIE and SIE in mstatus do not influence whether WFI stalls the hart or resumes execution.  
RULE 3: WFI must resume execution for any locally enabled interrupt that is pending at any privilege level, regardless of the values of the global interrupt enable bits MIE and SIE in mstatus.

The following description:

- Upon reset, a hart's privilege mode is set to M.  

- The **mstatus** fields **MIE** and **MPRV** are reset to 0.  

- If little‑endian memory accesses are supported, the **mstatus / mstatush** field **MBE** is reset to 0.

=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 2: Upon reset, the MPRV field in mstatus is reset to 0.  
RULE 3: If little‑endian memory accesses are supported, the MBE field in mstatus (or mstatush) is reset to 0.  
RULE 4: All mstatus bits not explicitly mentioned above have UNspecified values after reset.

The following description:

- The **mstatus** register contains the same fields as the **sstatus** register (the sstatus register is a subset of the mstatus register).

- In a straightforward implementation, **reading or writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to reading or writing the homonymous field in mstatus**.

- **SPP bit (in mstatus)**:  
  *When a trap is taken, SPP is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode, or 1 otherwise.*  
  *When an SRET instruction is executed to return from the trap handler, the privilege level is set to user mode if the SPP bit is 0, or supervisor mode if the SPP bit is 1; SPP is then set to 0.*

- **SIE bit (in mstatus)**:  
  *SIE enables or disables all interrupts in supervisor mode.*  
  *When SIE is clear, interrupts are not taken while in supervisor mode.*  
  *When the hart is running in user mode, the value in SIE is ignored, and supervisor‑level interrupts are enabled.*  
  *The supervisor can disable individual interrupt sources using the sie CSR.*

- **SPIE bit (in mstatus)**:  
  *SPIE indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode.*  
  *When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, SPIE is set to the current value of SIE, and SIE is set to 0.*  
  *When an SRET instruction is executed, SIE is set to the value of SPIE, then SPIE is set to 1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register contains the same fields as the sstatus register.  
RULE 2: In a straightforward implementation, reading any field in sstatus is equivalent to reading the homonymous field in mstatus.  
RULE 3: In a straightforward implementation, writing any field in sstatus is equivalent to writing the homonymous field in mstatus.  
RULE 4: When a trap is taken, the SPP field in mstatus is set to 0 if the trap originated from user mode.  
RULE 5: When a trap is taken, the SPP field in mstatus is set to 1 otherwise.  
RULE 6: When an SRET instruction is executed, the privilege level is set to user mode if the SPP field in mstatus is 0.  
RULE 7: When an SRET instruction is executed, the privilege level is set to supervisor mode if the SPP field in mstatus is 1.  
RULE 8: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPP field in mstatus is then set to 0.  
RULE 9: The SIE field in mstatus enables all interrupts in supervisor mode.  
RULE 10: The SIE field in mstatus disables all interrupts in supervisor mode when it is clear.  
RULE 11: When the hart is running in user mode, the value of the SIE field in mstatus is ignored.  
RULE 12: When the hart is running in user mode, supervisor‑level interrupts are enabled regardless of the SIE field in mstatus.  
RULE 13: The SPIE field in mstatus indicates whether supervisor interrupts were enabled prior to trapping into supervisor mode.  
RULE 14: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SPIE field in mstatus is set to the current value of the SIE field.  
RULE 15: When a trap is taken into supervisor mode, the SIE field in mstatus is set to 0.  
RULE 16: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SIE field in mstatus is set to the value of the SPIE field.  
RULE 17: When an SRET instruction is executed, the SPIE field in mstatus is then set to 1.
======================================================= END - 34 =======================================================

The following description:

- **Rule (from step 6 of the virtual‑address translation algorithm):**  
  *Condition:* When determining if the requested memory access is allowed by the `pte.u` bit, the check is performed **given the current privilege mode and the value of the SUM and MXR fields of the mstatus register**.  
  *Architectural effect:* If the access is **not** allowed under those conditions, the translation **stops and raises a page‑fault exception** corresponding to the original access type.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The virtual‑address translation algorithm must consider the current privilege mode together with the SUM field of mstatus when evaluating whether the pte.u bit permits the requested memory access.  
RULE 2: The virtual‑address translation algorithm must consider the current privilege mode together with the MXR field of mstatus when evaluating whether the pte.u bit permits the requested memory access.  
RULE 3: If, based on the current privilege mode and the values of the SUM and MXR fields of mstatus, the pte.u check does not permit the requested memory access, the translation must stop and raise a page‑fault exception of the original access type.
