The following description:

- **When executing a SINVAL.VMA instruction** in **S‑mode or HS‑mode**, if **mstatus.TVM = 1** then the instruction **raises an illegal‑instruction exception**.  

- **When executing a HINVAL.GVMA instruction** in **S‑mode or HS‑mode**, if **mstatus.TVM = 1** then the instruction **raises an illegal‑instruction exception**.  

- **SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions** are **unaffected by the mstatus.TVM field** (and the hstatus.VTVM field) **and hence are always permitted in S‑mode and VS‑mode**.  

- **SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions** **do not need to be trapped** when **mstatus.TVM = 1** (or when hstatus.VTVM = 1), because they only have ordering effects and no visible side effects.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When executing a SINVAL.VMA instruction in S‑mode or HS‑mode, if mstatus.TVM = 1 the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: When executing a HINVAL.GVMA instruction in S‑mode or HS‑mode, if mstatus.TVM = 1 the instruction raises an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions are unaffected by the mstatus.TVM field (and the hstatus.VTVM field) and are therefore always permitted in S‑mode and VS‑mode.  
RULE 4: SFENCE.W.INVAL and SFENCE.INVAL.IR instructions do not need to be trapped when mstatus.TVM = 1 (or when hstatus.VTVM = 1) because they only have ordering effects and no visible side effects.
======================================================= END - 37 =======================================================

The following description:

- The hypervisor extension adds two fields, **MPV** and **GVA**, to the machine‑level **mstatus** CSR (or to **mstatush** when MXLEN=32).  
- When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds **MPV** and **GVA** not to **mstatus** but to **mstatush**; the format of **mstatus** is unchanged for RV32.  

- **MPV (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode)** is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
- At the time of a trap, the MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode **V** at the time of the trap (analogous to MPP being set to the nominal privilege mode).  
- When an **MRET** instruction is executed, the virtualization mode **V** is set to MPV, **unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0**.  

- **GVA (Guest Virtual Address)** is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
- **For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest‑page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1**.  
- **For any other trap into M‑mode, GVA is set to 0**.  

- The **TSR** and **TVM** fields of **mstatus** **affect execution only in HS‑mode, not in VS‑mode**.  
- The **TW** field **affects execution in all modes except M‑mode**.  

- **Setting TVM=1 prevents HS‑mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA**, **but has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA**.  

- **Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS‑mode**.  

- The hypervisor extension **changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus**.  
  - **When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal**.  
  - **When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP**.  

- **Table 49 – Effect of MPRV on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses**  

  | MPRV | MPV | MPP | Architectural effect (as stated) |
  |------|-----|-----|-----------------------------------|
  | 0    | –   | –   | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
  | 1    | 0   | 0   | U‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only. |
  | 1    | 0   | 1   | HS‑level access with HS‑level translation and protection only. |
  | 1    | –   | 3   | M‑level access with no translation. |
  | 1    | 1   | 0   | VU‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection. The HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage. |
  | 1    | 1   | 1   | VS‑level access with two‑stage translation and protection. The HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage. vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS‑level SUM bit. |

- **MPRV does not affect the virtual‑machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV**.  

- **The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPV P, overriding MPRV**.  

- **The mstatus register is a superset of the HS‑level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to the machine‑level mstatus CSR.  
RULE 2: When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds the MPV and GVA fields to mstatush, leaving the format of mstatus unchanged for RV32.  
RULE 3: The MPV field in mstatus is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
RULE 4: At the time of a trap, the MPV bit in mstatus is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.  
RULE 5: When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to the MPV field in mstatus unless the MPP field in mstatus equals 3, in which case V remains 0.  
RULE 6: The GVA field in mstatus is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M‑mode.  
RULE 7: For any trap of type breakpoint, address‑misaligned, access‑fault, page‑fault, or guest‑page‑fault that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, the GVA bit in mstatus is set to 1.  
RULE 8: For any other trap into M‑mode, the GVA bit in mstatus is set to 0.  
RULE 9: The TSR field of mstatus affects execution only in HS‑mode and does not affect execution in VS‑mode.  
RULE 10: The TVM field of mstatus affects execution only in HS‑mode and does not affect execution in VS‑mode.  
RULE 11: The TW field of mstatus affects execution in all modes except M‑mode.  
RULE 12: Setting the TVM field in mstatus to 1 prevents HS‑mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA.  
RULE 13: Setting the TVM field in mstatus to 1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp or on execution of HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.  
RULE 14: Setting the TVM field in mstatus to 1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp, for execution of HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS‑mode.  
RULE 15: The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the MPRV field in mstatus.  
RULE 16: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 0, address translation and protection behave as normal.  
RULE 17: When the MPRV field in mstatus is 1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied as though the current virtualization mode were the MPV field in mstatus and the current nominal privilege mode were the MPP field in mstatus.  
RULE 18: When MPRV=0 in mstatus, explicit memory accesses are normal accesses with the current privilege mode applying.  
RULE 19: When MPRV=1, MPV=0, and MPP=0 in mstatus, explicit memory accesses are treated as U‑level accesses with HS‑level translation and protection only.  
RULE 20: When MPRV=1, MPV=0, and MPP=1 in mstatus, explicit memory accesses are treated as HS‑level accesses with HS‑level translation and protection only.  
RULE 21: When MPRV=1 and MPP=3 in mstatus (MPV value unspecified), explicit memory accesses are treated as M‑level accesses with no translation.  
RULE 22: When MPRV=1, MPV=1, and MPP=0 in mstatus, explicit memory accesses are treated as VU‑level accesses with two‑stage translation and protection; the HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable, and vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage.  
RULE 23: When MPRV=1, MPV=1, and MPP=1 in mstatus, explicit memory accesses are treated as VS‑level accesses with two‑stage translation and protection; the HS‑level MXR bit makes any executable page readable, vsstatus.MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage only if readable at the guest‑physical translation stage, and vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS‑level SUM bit.  
RULE 24: The MPRV field in mstatus does not affect the virtual‑machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.  
RULE 25: The explicit loads and stores of HLV, HLVX, and HSV act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPV P, overriding the MPRV field in mstatus.  
RULE 26: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS‑level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.

The following description:

- **HS‑qualified definition** – "An instruction is HS‑qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS‑mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), **assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero**."  
  *Condition*: mstatus.TSR = 0 **and** mstatus.TVM = 0.  
  *Effect*: Determines whether an instruction is considered HS‑qualified for the virtual‑instruction exception rule.

- **Virtual‑instruction exception when V=1** – "When V=1, a virtual‑instruction exception (code 22) is normally raised instead of an illegal‑instruction exception **if the attempted instruction is HS‑qualified** but is prevented from executing when V=1 either due to insufficient privilege or because the instruction is expressly disabled by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren."  
  *Condition*: V = 1 **and** the instruction is HS‑qualified (i.e., mstatus.TSR = 0 and mstatus.TVM = 0) **and** execution is prevented by privilege or by a CSR such as scounteren/hcounteren.  
  *Effect*: A virtual‑instruction exception (exception code 22) is taken instead of an illegal‑instruction exception.

- **Ignoring TSR and TVM in VS‑mode** – "Because TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S‑mode (HS‑mode), **they are ignored for determining exceptions in VS‑mode**."  
  *Condition*: Current mode is VS‑mode.  
  *Effect*: mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM have no effect on exception determination in VS‑mode.

- **WFI in VU‑mode** – "in VU‑mode, attempts to execute **WFI when mstatus.TW=0**, or to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE);"  
  *Condition*: Current mode is VU‑mode **and** mstatus.TW = 0 **and** the instruction is WFI (or a supervisor instruction SRET/SFENCE).  
  *Effect*: A virtual‑instruction exception is raised.

- **WFI in VS‑mode** – "in VS‑mode, attempts to execute **WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0**, unless the instruction completes within an implementation‑specific, bounded time;"  
  *Condition*: Current mode is VS‑mode **and** hstatus.VTW = 1 **and** mstatus.TW = 0 **and** the WFI instruction does not complete within the implementation‑specific bounded time.  
  *Effect*: A virtual‑instruction exception is raised.

These statements capture all explicit architectural behavior of the **mstatus** register (fields TSR, TVM, and TW) that is described in the provided text, together with the exact conditions and resulting effects.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An instruction is considered HS‑qualified for the virtual‑instruction exception rule only when the fields TSR and TVM of the CSR mstatus are both zero.  
RULE 2: When the V bit is 1, if an attempted instruction is HS‑qualified—meaning mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM are both zero—and execution is prevented by insufficient privilege or by a supervisor or hypervisor CSR such as scounteren or hcounteren, then a virtual‑instruction exception with code 22 is taken instead of an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: In VS‑mode, the fields TSR and TVM of the CSR mstatus have no effect on exception determination.  
RULE 4: In VU‑mode, if the field TW of the CSR mstatus is zero and a WFI instruction is executed, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised.  
RULE 5: In VU‑mode, if the field TW of the CSR mstatus is zero and a supervisor instruction such as SRET or SFENCE is executed, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised.  
RULE 6: In VS‑mode, if hstatus.VTW is 1 and the field TW of the CSR mstatus is zero and a WFI instruction does not complete within the implementation‑specific bounded time, a virtual‑instruction exception is raised.

The following description:

- When a trap occurs in HS‑mode or U‑mode, it goes to M‑mode, unless delegated by **medeleg** or **mideleg**.  
- When a trap occurs in VS‑mode or VU‑mode, it goes to M‑mode, unless delegated by **medeleg** or **mideleg**, in which case it goes to HS‑mode, unless further delegated by **hedeleg** or **hideleg**, in which case it goes to VS‑mode.  

**Effect on mstatus (or mstatush) when a trap is taken into M‑mode**  
- Virtualization mode **V** gets set to **0**.  
- Fields **MPV** and **MPP** in **mstatus** (or **mstatush**) are set according to Table 52.  
- Fields **GVA**, **MPIE**, and **MIE** in **mstatus** / **mstatush** are written.  

**Table 52. Value of mstatus / mstatush fields MPV and MPP after a trap into M‑mode**  

| Previous Mode | MPV | MPP |
|---------------|-----|-----|
| U‑mode        | 0   | 0   |
| HS‑mode       | 0   | 1   |
| M‑mode        | 0   | 3   |
| VU‑mode       | 1   | 0   |
| VS‑mode       | 1   | 1   |

- Upon trap return, **MPV** is ignored when **MPP = 3**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When a trap is taken into M‑mode, the virtualization mode field V in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0.  
RULE 2: If the previous privilege mode was U‑mode, then the MPV field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0.  
RULE 3: If the previous privilege mode was HS‑mode, then the MPV field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1.  
RULE 4: If the previous privilege mode was M‑mode, then the MPV field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0 and the MPP field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 3.  
RULE 5: If the previous privilege mode was VU‑mode, then the MPV field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 0.  
RULE 6: If the previous privilege mode was VS‑mode, then the MPV field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1 and the MPP field in mstatus (or mstatush) is set to 1.  
RULE 7: When a trap is taken into M‑mode, the GVA field in mstatus (or mstatush) is written.  
RULE 8: When a trap is taken into M‑mode, the MPIE field in mstatus (or mstatush) is written.  
RULE 9: When a trap is taken into M‑mode, the MIE field in mstatus (or mstatush) is written.  
RULE 10: Upon trap return, the MPV field in mstatus is ignored when the MPP field in mstatus equals 3.

The following description:

- An **MPELP bit** is provided in the **mstatus CSR** to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M‑mode.  
- An **SPELP bit** is provided in the **mstatus CSR** to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS‑mode.  
- The **SPELP bit in mstatus** can be accessed through the **sstatus CSR**.  
- When a trap is taken into privilege mode **x**, **the x PELP bit** (i.e., MPELP for M‑mode or SPELP for S/HS‑mode) **is set to ELP** and **ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED**.  
- When executing an **x RET** instruction (MRET or SRET):  
  - **If the new privilege mode is y**, **ELP is set to the value of x PELP** if **y LPE** is 1; otherwise **ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED**.  
  - **x PELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED**.  
- The trap handler in privilege mode **x must save the x PELP bit** (and the x7 register) before performing an indirect call/jump if **xLPE=1**.  
- If the privilege mode **x can respond to interrupts** and **xLPE=1**, then the trap handler **should also save these values** before enabling interrupts.  
- The trap handler in privilege mode **x must restore the saved x PELP bit** (and the x7 register) before executing the **x RET** instruction to return from a trap.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An MPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to M‑mode.  
RULE 2: An SPELP bit is provided in the mstatus CSR to store the previous ELP state on trap delivery to S/HS‑mode.  
RULE 3: The SPELP bit in mstatus is accessible through the sstatus CSR.  
RULE 4: When a trap is taken into privilege mode x, the x PELP bit in mstatus (MPELP for M‑mode or SPELP for S/HS‑mode) is set to the current ELP value and ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 5: If the new privilege mode after an x RET instruction is y, then ELP is set to the value of the x PELP bit in mstatus if y LPE is 1; otherwise ELP is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 6: When executing an x RET instruction, the x PELP bit in mstatus is set to NO_LP_EXPECTED.  
RULE 7: The trap handler in privilege mode x MUST save the x PELP bit in mstatus (and the x7 register) before performing an indirect call or jump if xLPE=1.  
RULE 8: If privilege mode x can respond to interrupts and xLPE=1, the trap handler SHOULD also save the x PELP bit in mstatus (and the x7 register) before enabling interrupts.  
RULE 9: The trap handler in privilege mode x MUST restore the saved x PELP bit in mstatus (and the x7 register) before executing the x RET instruction to return from a trap.

The following description:

- **When TVM = 1** (TVM is a WARL field in the *mstatus* register), **any attempt to read or write the satp CSR while executing in S‑mode** **MUST** raise an *illegal‑instruction exception*.

- **When TVM = 1**, **any attempt to execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S‑mode** **MUST** raise an *illegal‑instruction exception* (the same condition that traps satp accesses).

- **When TVM = 0**, the operations listed above (read/write satp CSR, SFENCE.VMA, SINVAL.VMA) **are permitted in S‑mode** (i.e., they do **not** raise an illegal‑instruction exception.

- **TVM is read‑only 0 when S‑mode is not supported** (i.e., the hardware must present TVM=0 in implementations lacking S‑mode).

- **Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the hooks necessary to lazily populate shadow page tables** (descriptive statement about the purpose of the SATP trap mechanism).
============================================================

RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 1: When the TVM field in mstatus equals 1, any attempt to read or write the satp CSR while executing in S‑mode MUST raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 2: When TVM equals 1, the same condition that traps satp CSR accesses also requires that execution of an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction in S‑mode MUST raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 3: When TVM equals 0, reads or writes of the satp CSR in S‑mode do not raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 4: Trapping satp accesses and the SFENCE.VMA and SINVAL.VMA instructions provides the architectural hooks needed to lazily populate shadow page tables.

The following description:

- If **S-mode is not supported**, or if **satp.MODE is read‑only zero** (always Bare), the implementation **may make FIOM read‑only zero**.  

- The **PBMTE bit** in **menvcfg** **controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for use in S‑mode and G‑stage address translation (i.e., for page tables pointed to by satp or hgatp)**.  
  - When **PBMTE=1**, Svpbmt **is available** for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation.  
  - When **PBMTE=0**, the implementation **behaves as though Svpbmt were not implemented**.  
  - If **Svpbmt is not implemented**, **PBMTE is read‑only zero**.  
  - Furthermore, for implementations with the hypervisor extension, **henvcfg.PBMTE is read‑only zero if menvcfg.PBMTE is zero**.

- The **ADUE bit** in **menvcfg** **controls whether hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled for S‑mode and G‑stage address translations** (i.e., for page tables pointed to by satp).  
  - When **ADUE=1**, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits **is enabled during S‑mode address translation**, and the implementation **behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for S‑mode address translation**.  
  - When the hypervisor extension is implemented, **if ADUE=1**, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits **is enabled during G‑stage address translation**, and the implementation **behaves as though the Svade extension were not implemented for G‑stage address translation**.  
  - When **ADUE=0**, the implementation **behaves as though Svade were implemented** for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation.  
  - If **Svadu is not implemented**, **ADUE is read‑only zero**.  
  - Furthermore, for implementations with the hypervisor extension, **henvcfg.ADUE is read‑only zero if menvcfg.ADUE is zero**.  

- The **Svade extension** **requires page‑fault exceptions be raised when PTE A/D bits need be set**, **hence Svade is implemented when ADUE=0**.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If S-mode is not supported, or if the satp MODE field is read‑only zero (always Bare), the implementation may make the FIOM register read‑only zero.  
RULE 2: The PBMTE bit in menvcfg controls whether the Svpbmt extension is available for address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 3: When PBMTE=1, the Svpbmt extension is available for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 4: When PBMTE=0, the implementation behaves as though the Svpbmt extension is not implemented for address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 5: If the Svpbmt extension is not implemented, the PBMTE bit is read‑only zero.  
RULE 6: For implementations that include the hypervisor extension, if menvcfg.PBMTE is zero then henvcfg.PBMTE is read‑only zero, affecting address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 7: The ADUE bit in menvcfg controls whether hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled for address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 8: When ADUE=1, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during S‑mode address translation of page tables referenced by satp, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is not implemented for S‑mode address translation.  
RULE 9: When the hypervisor extension is implemented and ADUE=1, hardware updating of PTE A/D bits is enabled during G‑stage address translation of page tables referenced by satp, and the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is not implemented for G‑stage address translation.  
RULE 10: When ADUE=0, the implementation behaves as though the Svade extension is implemented for S‑mode and G‑stage address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 11: If the Svadu extension is not implemented, the ADUE bit is read‑only zero.  
RULE 12: For implementations with the hypervisor extension, if menvcfg.ADUE is zero then henvcfg.ADUE is read‑only zero, influencing address translation of page tables referenced by satp.  
RULE 13: The Svade extension requires page‑fault exceptions to be raised when PTE A/D bits need to be set, and therefore the Svade extension is considered implemented when ADUE=0 for address translation of page tables referenced by satp.
======================================================= END - 53 =======================================================

The following description:

- **Register definition**  
  *The satp CSR is an SXLEN‑bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 63 for SXLEN=32 and Figure 64 for SXLEN=64, which controls supervisor‑mode address translation and protection.*  

- **Fields of satp**  
  *This register holds the physical page number (PPN) of the root page table, an address‑space identifier (ASID), and the MODE field, which selects the current address‑translation scheme.*  

- **Figure 63 – satp when SXLEN=32**  

  | Bits | Field | Type |
  |------|-------|------|
  | 31   | MODE  | WARL |
  | 30‑22| ASID  | WARL |
  | 21‑0 | PPN   | WARL |

- **Figure 64 – satp when SXLEN=64**  

  | Bits | Field | Type |
  |------|-------|------|
  | 63‑60| MODE  | WARL |
  | 59‑44| ASID  | WARL |
  | 43‑0 | PPN   | WARL |

- **PPN storage**  
  *Storing a PPN in satp, rather than a physical address, supports a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32.*  
  *The satp.PPN field might not be capable of holding all physical page numbers; some platform standards might place constraints on the values satp.PPN may assume, e.g., by requiring that all physical page numbers corresponding to main memory be representable.*

- **Atomic update rationale**  
  *We store the ASID and the page table base address in the same CSR to allow the pair to be changed atomically on a context switch. Swapping them non‑atomically could pollute the old virtual address space with new translations, or vice‑versa. This approach also slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.*

- **MODE = Bare behavior**  
  *When MODE = Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses, and there is no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme described in Section 3.7.*  
  *To select MODE = Bare, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp (bits 30‑0 when SXLEN=32, or bits 59‑0 when SXLEN=64).*  
  *Attempting to select MODE = Bare with a nonzero pattern in the remaining fields has an **UNSPECIFIED** effect on the value that the remaining fields assume and an **UNSPECIFIED** effect on address translation and protection behavior.*

- **Custom and reserved encodings for MODE = Bare**  
  *When SXLEN=32, the satp encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use, whereas the encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7]≠3 are reserved for future standard use.*  
  *When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings corresponding to MODE = Bare are reserved for future standard use.*

- **Valid MODE values**  
  *When SXLEN=32, the only other valid setting for MODE is Sv32, a paged virtual‑memory scheme described in Section 12.3.*  
  *When SXLEN=64, three paged virtual‑memory schemes are defined: Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57 (described in Sections 12.4‑12.6). One additional scheme, Sv64, will be defined in a later version of this specification. The remaining MODE settings are reserved for future use and may define different interpretations of the other fields in satp.*

- **Unsupported MODE writes**  
  *Implementations are not required to support all MODE settings, and if satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect; no fields in satp are modified.*

- **ASID length**  
  *The number of ASID bits is **UNSPECIFIED** and may be zero.*  
  *The number of implemented ASID bits, termed ASIDLEN, may be determined by writing one to every bit position in the ASID field, then reading back the value in satp to see which bit positions in the ASID field hold a one.*  
  *The least‑significant bits of ASID are implemented first: that is, if ASIDLEN > 0, ASID[ASIDLEN‑1:0] is writable.*  
  *The maximal value of ASIDLEN, termed ASIDMAX, is 9 for Sv32 or 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.*

- **Table 40 – Encoding of satp MODE field**

  *SXLEN=32*  

  | Value | Name   | Description                                          |
  |-------|--------|------------------------------------------------------|
  | 0 1   | Bare Sv32 | No translation or protection. Page‑based 32‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.3). |

  *SXLEN=64*  

  | Value | Name | Description                                                  |
  |-------|------|--------------------------------------------------------------|
  | 0     | Bare | No translation or protection.                               |
  | 1‑7   | –    | Reserved for standard use                                   |
  | 8     | Sv39 | Page‑based 39‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.4).   |
  | 9     | Sv48 | Page‑based 48‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.5).   |
  | 10    | Sv57 | Page‑based 57‑bit virtual addressing (see Section 12.6).   |
  | 11    | Sv64 | Reserved for page‑based 64‑bit virtual addressing.          |
  | 12‑13 | –    | Reserved for standard use                                   |
  | 14‑15 | –    | Designated for custom use                                    |

- **Activation condition**  
  *The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S‑mode or U‑mode.*  
  *Executions of the address‑translation algorithm may only begin using a given value of satp when satp is active.*

- **Translations after satp deactivation**  
  *Translations that began while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp is no longer active, unless an SFENCE.VMA instruction matching the address and ASID is executed.*  
  *The SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to the address‑translation data structures are observed by subsequent implicit reads to those structures by a hart.*

- **Ordering and cache effects of satp writes**  
  *Writing satp does not imply any ordering constraints between page‑table updates and subsequent address translations, nor does it imply any invalidation of address‑translation caches.*  
  *If the new address space's page tables have been modified, or if an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.*  
  *Not imposing upon implementations to flush address‑translation caches upon satp writes reduces the cost of context switches, provided a sufficiently large ASID space.*
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The satp CSR is an SXLEN‑bit read/write register that controls supervisor‑mode address translation and protection and its layout follows the formats specified for SXLEN=32 and SXLEN=64.  
RULE 2: The satp register contains a physical page number (PPN) field for the root page table, an address‑space identifier (ASID) field, and a MODE field that selects the current address‑translation scheme.  
RULE 3: For SXLEN=32, bits 31 of satp constitute the MODE field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 4: For SXLEN=32, bits 30‑22 of satp constitute the ASID field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 5: For SXLEN=32, bits 21‑0 of satp constitute the PPN field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 6: For SXLEN=64, bits 63‑60 of satp constitute the MODE field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 7: For SXLEN=64, bits 59‑44 of satp constitute the ASID field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 8: For SXLEN=64, bits 43‑0 of satp constitute the PPN field, which is a WARL field.  
RULE 9: Storing a PPN in satp rather than a full physical address enables a physical address space larger than 4 GiB for RV32 implementations.  
RULE 10: The satp.PPN field may be unable to represent all physical page numbers, and platform standards may impose constraints on the values that satp.PPN may assume.  
RULE 11: The ASID and page‑table base address are stored together in satp to permit their atomic update on a context switch.  
RULE 12: Non‑atomic swapping of the ASID and page‑table base address could cause old virtual address spaces to be polluted with new translations, or vice‑versa.  
RULE 13: Storing ASID and the page‑table base in satp also slightly reduces the cost of a context switch.  
RULE 14: When the MODE field of satp is Bare, supervisor virtual addresses are equal to supervisor physical addresses and no additional memory protection beyond the physical memory protection scheme is provided.  
RULE 15: To select Bare mode, software must write zero to the remaining fields of satp (bits 30‑0 for SXLEN=32 or bits 59‑0 for SXLEN=64).  
RULE 16: Writing a nonzero pattern to the remaining fields of satp while selecting Bare mode has an UNSPECIFIED effect on the value that those fields assume.  
RULE 17: Writing a nonzero pattern to the remaining fields of satp while selecting Bare mode also has an UNSPECIFIED effect on address‑translation and protection behavior.  
RULE 18: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings with MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7]=3 are designated for custom use.  
RULE 19: When SXLEN=32, satp encodings with MODE = Bare and ASID[8:7]≠3 are reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 20: When SXLEN=64, all satp encodings with MODE = Bare are reserved for future standard use.  
RULE 21: For SXLEN=32, the only other defined MODE value for satp is Sv32.  
RULE 22: For SXLEN=64, the defined paged virtual‑memory MODE values for satp are Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57.  
RULE 23: For SXLEN=64, a future Sv64 MODE for satp will be defined in a later version of the specification.  
RULE 24: All other MODE settings of satp are reserved for future use and may assign different interpretations to the remaining satp fields.  
RULE 25: Implementations are not required to support every MODE value, and if satp is written with an unsupported MODE, the entire write has no effect and no satp fields are modified.  
RULE 26: The number of ASID bits represented in satp is UNSPECIFIED and may be zero.  
RULE 27: The implemented number of ASID bits in satp, called ASIDLEN, can be discovered by writing ones to every bit position of the ASID field and reading back which bits retain a one.  
RULE 28: If ASIDLEN > 0, the least‑significant ASIDLEN bits of the satp ASID field are writable.  
RULE 29: The maximum possible ASIDLEN value, ASIDMAX, is 9 for Sv32 and 16 for Sv39, Sv48, and Sv57 as encoded in satp.  
RULE 30: The satp CSR is considered active when the effective privilege mode is S‑mode or U‑mode.  
RULE 31: Address‑translation algorithm executions may only begin using a given satp value when satp is active.  
RULE 32: Translations that started while satp was active are not required to complete or terminate when satp becomes inactive, unless a matching SFENCE.VMA instruction is executed.  
RULE 33: An SFENCE.VMA instruction must be used to ensure that updates to address‑translation data structures become visible to subsequent implicit reads by a hart.  
RULE 34: Writing satp does not impose any ordering constraints between page‑table updates and later address translations, nor does it cause any invalidation of address‑translation caches.  
RULE 35: If the new address space's page tables have been modified or an ASID is reused, it may be necessary to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction after, or in some cases before, writing satp.  
RULE 36: Not requiring implementations to flush address‑translation caches on satp writes reduces context‑switch cost, provided the ASID space is sufficiently large.

The following description:

Implementations must only perform implicit reads of the translation data structures pointed to by the **current contents of the satp register** or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation‑data‑structure entry, and must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not those that are performed speculatively.

Changes to **satp.MODE** from Bare to other modes and vice versa also take effect immediately, without the need to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction.

Changes to **satp.ASID** take effect immediately.

When software recycles an ASID (i.e., reassociates it with a different page table), it should first **change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID**, then execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.  
Alternatively, software can execute the same SFENCE.VMA instruction while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided the next time satp is loaded with the recycled ASID, it is simultaneously loaded with the new page table.

If the implementation does not provide ASIDs, or software chooses to always use ASID 0, then after **every satp write**, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0. In the common case that no global translations have been modified, rs2 should be set to a register other than x0 but which contains the value zero, so that global translations are not flushed.

If software modifies a non‑leaf PTE, it should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0. If any PTE along the traversal path had its G bit set, rs2 **must be x0**; otherwise, rs2 **should be set to the ASID** for which the translation is being modified.

If software modifies a leaf PTE, it should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 **set to a virtual address within the page**. If any PTE along the traversal path had its G bit set, rs2 **must be x0**; otherwise, rs2 **should be set to the ASID** for which the translation is being modified.

For implementations that make **satp.MODE read‑only zero (always Bare)**, attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction **might raise an illegal‑instruction exception**.

The property that an implementation may use any translation that was valid at any time since the most recent subsuming SFENCE.VMA **applies even if the virtual‑address width for that translation differs from the width currently specified by satp.MODE**. For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, even if that translation used a virtual address of a different width. Similarly, for a given virtual address, any global translation since the last subsuming SFENCE.VMA might be used, regardless of both ASID and virtual‑address width.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: Implementations must only perform implicit reads of translation data structures pointed to by the current contents of satp or a subsequent valid (V=1) translation‑data‑structure entry.  
RULE 2: Implementations must only raise exceptions for implicit accesses that are generated as a result of instruction execution, not for those performed speculatively.  
RULE 3: Changes to satp.MODE from Bare to another mode or from another mode to Bare take effect immediately and do not require execution of an SFENCE.VMA instruction.  
RULE 4: Changes to satp.ASID take effect immediately.  
RULE 5: When software recycles an ASID, it should first change satp to point to the new page table using the recycled ASID before executing SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0 and rs2 set to the recycled ASID.  
RULE 6: Software may execute SFENCE.VMA while a different ASID is loaded into satp, provided that the next load of satp with the recycled ASID also loads the new page table.  
RULE 7: If the implementation does not provide ASIDs or software always uses ASID 0, software should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0 after every satp write.  
RULE 8: In the common case where no global translations have been modified after a satp write, software should set rs2 to a register other than x0 that holds zero so that global translations are not flushed.  
RULE 9: When software modifies a non‑leaf PTE, it should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 = x0, and rs2 must be x0 if any PTE on the traversal path has its G bit set; otherwise rs2 should be set to the ASID that would appear in satp for the modified translation.  
RULE 10: When software modifies a leaf PTE, it should execute SFENCE.VMA with rs1 set to a virtual address within the page, and rs2 must be x0 if any PTE on the traversal path has its G bit set; otherwise rs2 should be set to the ASID that would appear in satp for the modified translation.  
RULE 11: For implementations where satp.MODE is read‑only zero (always Bare), attempts to execute an SFENCE.VMA instruction might raise an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 12: For a given virtual address and ASID, any translation that was valid at any time since the most recent subsuming SFENCE.VMA may be used even if that translation employed a virtual‑address width different from the width currently specified by satp.MODE.  
RULE 13: For a given virtual address, any global translation that was valid at any time since the most recent subsuming SFENCE.VMA may be used regardless of both ASID and virtual‑address width.

- When **Sv32** is written to the **MODE** field in the **satp** register (see Section 12.1.11), the supervisor operates in a 32‑bit paged virtual‑memory system.

The following description:

- When the **MODE** field of the **satp** register is set to **Sv32**, supervisor virtual addresses are translated into supervisor physical addresses via a two‑level page table.  The 20‑bit virtual‑page number (VPN) is translated into a 22‑bit physical‑page number (PPN), while the 12‑bit page offset is untranslated.  The resulting supervisor‑level physical addresses are then checked by any physical‑memory‑protection structures before being directly converted to machine‑level physical addresses.  If necessary, these supervisor‑level physical addresses are zero‑extended to the number of physical‑address bits implemented in the hardware.

- When the **MODE** field of the **satp** register is set to **Bare**, the 32‑bit virtual address is translated (unmodified) into a 32‑bit physical address, and that physical address is zero‑extended into the machine‑level physical address width of the implementation.

- Example (descriptive): On an RV32 system that implements 34 physical‑address bits, if **satp.MODE = Sv32** a 34‑bit physical address is produced directly, so no zero‑extension is needed.  If **satp.MODE = Bare**, the 32‑bit virtual address is first used as a 32‑bit physical address and then zero‑extended to 34 bits.

- The **physical page number (PPN) of the root page table** is stored in the **satp** register.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the MODE field of satp is set to Sv32, supervisor virtual addresses are translated to supervisor physical addresses using a two-level page table.  
RULE 2: When satp.MODE is Sv32, the 20‑bit virtual‑page number is translated into a 22‑bit physical‑page number while the 12‑bit page offset remains unchanged.  
RULE 3: When satp.MODE is Sv32, the supervisor‑level physical addresses produced are examined by any physical‑memory‑protection structures before being directly converted to machine‑level physical addresses.  
RULE 4: When satp.MODE is Sv32, if required, the supervisor‑level physical addresses are zero‑extended to match the number of physical‑address bits implemented in the hardware.  
RULE 5: When satp.MODE is Bare, the 32‑bit virtual address is used unmodified as a 32‑bit physical address.  
RULE 6: When satp.MODE is Bare, the 32‑bit physical address derived from the virtual address is zero‑extended to the machine‑level physical address width of the implementation.  
RULE 7: On an RV32 implementation that provides 34 physical‑address bits, when satp.MODE is Sv32 a 34‑bit physical address is produced directly without requiring zero‑extension.  
RULE 8: On an RV32 implementation that provides 34 physical‑address bits, when satp.MODE is Bare the 32‑bit virtual address is first treated as a 32‑bit physical address and then zero‑extended to 34 bits.  
RULE 9: The physical page number of the root page table is stored in satp.

The following description:

- **Step 0 of the translation algorithm**:  
  *Let a be **satp.ppn × PAGESIZE**, and let i = LEVELS‑1. (For Sv32, PAGESIZE = 2¹² and LEVELS = 2.)*  
  This statement defines that the physical‑page‑number field (ppn) of the **satp** register is multiplied by the page size to form the base address *a* used for the first page‑table walk.

- **Satp‑activity requirement**:  
  *The **satp** register **must be active**, i.e., the effective privilege mode must be S‑mode or U‑mode.*  
  The translation algorithm may be executed only when **satp** is considered active, which is explicitly defined as the processor being in supervisor or user mode.

- **Root page‑table source**:  
  *The algorithm may only touch page tables reachable from the root page table indicated in **satp**, the range of addresses that an implementation's page‑table walker will touch is fully under supervisor control.*  
  The root of the page‑table hierarchy is explicitly taken from the value held in **satp**; therefore all page‑table accesses are limited to the region described by that root pointer.

- **Speculative execution condition**:  
  *Implementations may also execute the address‑translation algorithm speculatively at any time, for any virtual address, as long as **satp** is active (as defined in Section 12.1.11).*  
  Speculative walks of the page‑table structures are permitted only when the same **satp‑activity** condition (effective privilege mode S‑mode or U‑mode) holds.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The satp register must be active, which requires the effective privilege mode to be S-mode or U-mode.  
RULE 2: The translation algorithm may be executed only when the satp register is active (effective privilege mode S-mode or U-mode).  
RULE 3: The base address a for the first page‑table walk is computed as the satp register's physical‑page‑number field multiplied by the page size.  
RULE 4: The page‑table walker may access only page‑table pages that are reachable from the root page‑table address supplied in the satp register.  
RULE 5: The address range accessed by the page‑table walker, derived from the root page‑table indicated in satp, is fully under supervisor control.  
RULE 6: Implementations may speculatively execute the address‑translation algorithm for any virtual address at any time provided that the satp register is active (effective privilege mode S-mode or U-mode).

The following description:

When V=1, vsatp substitutes for the usual satp, so instructions that normally read or modify satp actually access vsatp instead.

When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored as it is for satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.

When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored and no write to vsatp is effected.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, any instruction that reads or modifies the satp register accesses the vsatp register instead.  
RULE 2: When V=0, a write to vsatp with an unsupported MODE value is either ignored as it is for satp, or the fields of vsatp are treated as WARL in the normal way.  
RULE 3: When V=1, a write to satp with an unsupported MODE value is ignored and no write to vsatp is effected.

The following description:

- **Substitution of hgatp for satp** – "hgatp substitutes for the usual satp" when performing G‑stage address translation.  
- **Algorithmic equivalence** – The conversion of an Sv32x4, Sv39x4, Sv48x4, or Sv57x4 guest physical address is accomplished with the same algorithm used for Sv32, Sv39, Sv48, or Sv57 (the algorithm defined for the satp‑controlled translation), except for the following explicit differences:  
  - the effective privilege mode must be VS‑mode or VU‑mode for the translation to begin;  
  - when checking the U bit, the current privilege mode is always taken to be U‑mode;  
  - guest‑page‑fault exceptions are raised instead of regular page‑fault exceptions.  

These statements are the only explicit information about the satp register contained in the provided text.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hgatp register substitutes for the satp register when performing G‑stage address translation.  
RULE 2: The conversion of an Sv32x4, Sv39x4, Sv48x4, or Sv57x4 guest physical address is performed with the same algorithm defined for satp‑controlled translation of Sv32, Sv39, Sv48, or Sv57 addresses, except for the explicit differences listed.  
RULE 3: When the satp‑controlled algorithm is used for G‑stage address translation, the effective privilege mode must be VS‑mode or VU‑mode for the translation to begin.  
RULE 4: When the satp‑controlled algorithm checks the U bit during G‑stage address translation, the current privilege mode is always taken to be U‑mode.  
RULE 5: When the satp‑controlled algorithm encounters a page‑fault during G‑stage address translation, a guest‑page‑fault exception is raised instead of a regular page‑fault exception.

The following description:

- **Condition:** "all cases except when the active **satp** register's **MODE** field != Bare".  
  **Effect:** The ignore transformation that is applied to a physical address (including guest‑physical addresses) replaces the upper **PMLEN** bits with 0. ============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================

RULE 2: When the active satp register's MODE field does not equal Bare, the ignore transformation is not applied to a physical address (including guest‑physical addresses), so the upper PMLEN bits are not replaced with 0.

The following description:

- **scounteren is a 32‑bit CSR that controls the availability of the hardware performance‑monitoring counters to U‑mode.**  
  *Source:* "The counter‑enable ( scounteren ) CSR is a 32‑bit register that controls the availability of the hardware performance monitoring counters to U-mode."

- **scounteren must be implemented.**  
  *Source:* "scounteren must be implemented."

- **Each bit in scounteren may be read‑only zero; a read‑only‑zero bit indicates that reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U‑mode. Hence, the bits are effectively WARL fields.**  
  *Source:* "any of the bits may be read‑only zero, indicating reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U‑mode. Hence, they are effectively WARL fields."

- **When the CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in scounteren is clear, attempts to read the corresponding counter register (cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern) while executing in U‑mode will cause an illegal‑instruction exception.**  
  *Source:* "When the CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in the scounteren register is clear, attempts to read the cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern register while executing in U-mode will cause an illegal‑instruction exception."

- **When the CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in scounteren is set, access to the corresponding counter register (cycle, time, instret, or hpmcountern) is permitted.**  
  *Source:* "When one of these bits is set, access to the corresponding register is permitted."

- **The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.**  
  *Source:* "The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable."

- **U‑mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in both scounteren and mcounteren are set.**  
  *Source:* "U-mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in scounteren and mcounteren are both set."
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: scounteren is a 32‑bit CSR that controls the availability of the hardware performance‑monitoring counters to U‑mode.  
RULE 2: scounteren must be implemented.  
RULE 3: Any bit in scounteren may be read‑only zero.  
RULE 4: A read‑only‑zero bit in scounteren indicates that reads to the corresponding counter will cause an exception when executing in U‑mode.  
RULE 5: The bits in scounteren are effectively WARL fields.  
RULE 6: When a CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in scounteren is clear, attempts to read the corresponding counter register while executing in U‑mode will cause an illegal‑instruction exception.  
RULE 7: When a CY, TM, IR, or HPM n bit in scounteren is set, access to the corresponding counter register is permitted.  
RULE 8: The setting of a bit in mcounteren does not affect whether the corresponding bit in scounteren is writable.  
RULE 9: U‑mode may only access a counter if the corresponding bits in both scounteren and mcounteren are set.
